Naming `subagent` needs `tools.native.subagent.max_depth` of 2 or more, naming `tool_smith` needs `tools.native.tool_smith` in the session; otherwise the session fails to load with an error that says which. **Config, not visible to the model:** `tools.native.subagent.max_depth` (default 2, 1 to 8; 1 = only the session starts children), `max_runs_per_root` (default 16, 1 to 1024: runs going below one top-level run), `max_runs_per_session` (default 32, 1 to 1024: runs of the session at any depth, counted when a child starts one). `ExecutionContext.AgentDepth` (0 in the session, 1 in a child) and `RootRun` carry the position; a child's `SessionKey` is the session's, so a nested run belongs to the session for the limits, the restore guard and delete. **Fail fast:** a start from inside a child never queues; with no free slot it answers `subagent_busy`, beyond the depth `subagent_depth_exceeded`, beyond a run cap `subagent_limit_reached` (a queued parent that waits for its own queued child could otherwise never be freed). **Ownership:** a handle a child starts is owned by the child's run id (its `async` sees only those); a child without the async tool gets no handle and the call runs to its end. Run ids stay flat (`sub_` and 8 hex digits, fixed length), so the prefix cancel of a run id cannot reach another run; the tree is kept by contexts, not by names: **a nested run's context is a child of its parent run's context**, so a stop, reset, delete, timeout or the end of the parent ends every descendant, and the parent's end also removes the handles it owned (`DeleteOwnersWithPrefix`). Stop stays non-blocking. A writer-capable run (`background_writers`) is a handle only at depth 0. **Approval, unchanged:** a child still has no approval requester, so a nested tool whose `approval` is `ask` answers `approval_unavailable` ("the run has no way to ask") and does not run; a nested `tool_smith` runs in the child's call (scripts still cannot call `subagent`/`tool_smith`, D5). `profile_message` in a child is step 2. Tests: `subagent_nesting_test.go`; flipped on purpose: `TestToolSmithIsNeverInASubagentPool` (now `...ButOnlyAnAgentThatListsItGetsIt`) and the `subagent`/`tool_smith` cases of `TestSubagentToolsListNarrowsAndRejectsAnUnavailableName`.

- **Tool timeouts are capped at 6 hours (2026-10-23, user decision).** No timeout, block or wait setting of a tool accepts more than 21600000 ms; a larger value is a config error (the existing out-of-range convention, not a clamp). Capped: `tools.native.bash|exec|tool_smith` `timeout_ms` and `max_timeout_ms` (maximum was 3600000), `tools.native.profile_message` `timeout_ms` and `busy_wait_ms` (3600000), each sub-agent `timeout_ms` in setting.json and agent.json (3600000), and `tools.native.async` `default_block_ms`, `max_block_ms`, `default_wait_ms`, `max_wait_ms`, `stop_wait_ms` (10000000). Defaults are unchanged. Left alone because their maximum is already below 6 hours: glob, grep (10 min), fetch (5 min), request_draft (30 min), checkpoint and provider `idle_timeout_ms` (1 hour), hooks (10 min), extension decide (5 min), MCP servers (10 min), provider retry delay, `kill_*` and drain settings (1 min), `index_flush_ms`, and the `*_days` retention settings. The per-call `timeout_ms`, `block_ms` and `wait_ms` arguments are still clamped to these settings.

- **tool_smith input is flat (2026-10-23, user decision; supersedes the `run` object of the async-manager entry).** The model-facing schema has no `run` object any more. The code is the top-level string `function`; `input` (object) and `block_ms` (integer, 0 or more) sit beside it, and `filters` still lists. Nothing is nested, so `function` can no longer be given without its parameters being reachable. A call with no `function` lists the saved functions (an empty call still lists; `filters` narrows). A call with `function` is serialized and checkpointed (`MayChangeFiles`), as a call with `run` was. A `run` key is refused by the schema (`additionalProperties: false`) and the error says "run is not a parameter: pass the code as the top-level function string" with the example `{"function":"return 1;"}`; this uses the new optional `tools.Tool.RejectedProperties`, which replaces the generic "must NOT have additional properties" for a named key. The forgiving forms are gone: the plain-string `run`, and the aliases `script`, `code` and `body` (each is now an unknown key). `NormalizeArguments` only fixes a negative or fractional `block_ms`. `function` given with `filters` still runs the function and notes `ignored: filters`. Saved functions are still called as `fn.<name>(input)` inside a function body. The definition `sample/tools/native/tool_smith.json` and `sample/schemes` text changed to match; the schema, the description and the manifest must still match exactly. No config key changed.

- **The approval system is removed (2026-10-23, user decision; design_nesting_and_approval.md part B; B-1 to B-4 done, B-5 to B-7 follow).** Every enabled tool runs at once; JAD asks nobody before a call. The user's container is the security boundary: JAD is meant to run inside one that holds only the project and no secrets in its environment. The `paths` allow and deny lists and `tools.deny` remain as in-process limits, and they are not a sandbox (bash, exec and tool_smith can bypass path lists). `request_draft` is the only question left: it keeps the interaction types, the broker, `/run/interaction` and its own `headless_auto_accept_timeout_ms`. The draft is the only question kind.
  - **Removed keys are refused for good (D4).** `approval` in the settings (any value, including the whole `approval` section and `approval.default`), on `tools.native.write`, `edit`, `bash`, `exec`, `process`, `tool_smith` and `profile_message`, on an `mcp_servers[]` entry and on a toolset's `mcp` use is a configuration error that names the key ("tools.native.bash.approval was removed; …delete the key"). It is never ignored, because an ignored `approval: ask` would leave the user believing a protection is on. The list lives in `sessionfs/removed_keys.go` and is not meant to be deleted. `sessionfs` has no `ApprovalSettings`, `ApprovalOff` or `ApprovalAsk` any more, and `MCPServerReference` has no `Approval`.
  - **Sub-agents and scripts (B-2).** A child gets no approval requester. `background_writers` (default off) and `max_background_writers` stay: a writer is a handle only when the session turned them on, because the checkpoint still covers a run that ends with the call. They no longer look at any tool's approval. A script is always a handle; there is no synchronous fallback. An agent may list an MCP tool from any server; the old "server asks for approval" load errors and the wildcard skip are gone. The checkpoint restore guard (409 while a background writer runs) is unchanged (D6).
  - **Tools package (B-3).** `tools.ApprovalCall`, the requester, the preview, `CallNeedsApproval`, `Tool.Approval`, `ExecutionContext.RequestApproval`, the catalog gate and the error codes `approval_denied`, `approval_timeout`, `approval_cancelled` and `approval_unavailable` are deleted, as is `session.Options.Approval`. `CutText` moved to `tools/text.go`. `checkpoint` decides locally whether a `process` call changes things: a non-blank `command` does.
  - **Tests and fixtures.** The 13 approval cases of `testdata/layout-settings.cases.json` are removed (136 remain) and the removed keys have their own tests (`removed_keys_test.go`, the toolset and schema tests). `sample/schemes/setting.schema.json` and `toolset.schema.json` no longer have an approval section or key; `sample/jad/tools/coding/full.json` no longer sets one.
  - **Superseded.** The earlier chapters on approval below describe the removed behaviour and are kept as history until B-7 rewrites them.
- **`allow_write` is removed (2026-10-23, user decision; design_nesting_and_approval.md part B, B-4b).** It existed only so a write-capable child could be gated behind approvals. `tools.native.subagent.allow_write` is now a configuration error naming the key (any value), like the approval keys. An agent's `tools` list alone decides what its child gets: `write`, `edit`, `bash`, `exec` and `process` are given when the list names them and the session enables them (a name the session does not enable is still the "not an enabled native tool" error). An agent with no list still gets only the read-only tools. `childToolPools` always returns the change tools in the second pool, a call to an agent that lists one is still a change (checkpoint, ordered scheduling, `ChangesFiles`), and `background_writers` still decides whether such a call may be a handle. `profile_message`, `async`, `tool_smith` and `subagent` were already governed by the list alone. The `sample/schemes` descriptions lost the `allow_write` text and the stale "depth is one" sentence.

- **tool_smith input and output model (2026-10-24, user decision; changes the flat input above).** The script parameter is renamed `function` -> `code` (flat string; `input` and `block_ms` stay flat; no params or only `filters` still lists the saved functions; `MayChangeFiles` keys on `code`). `function` and `run` are both refused through `tools.Tool.RejectedProperties` with a message naming the top-level `code` string and the example `{"code":"console.log(1);"}`. No alias is accepted. Saved functions are still called `fn.<name>(input)`. **Output:** stdout is the primary result of a script; `return` stays optional and only for structured values. A returned value is still shown in a `[result]` block; a body that returns nothing gives no `[result]` block and its stdout is the result. This needed no runtime change (the prelude already wrote the result file only for a defined return value); the description and schema text say so. `sample/tools/native/tool_smith.json` and `design_tool_smith.md` match. Local `.jad` skill text and profiles that show `"function"` examples are not tracked and not changed.
- **A child's `profile_message` reaches the target with the target's own tools and paths (2026-10-23).** The message runs as an ordinary run of the target profile, so the target works with its own full tool set and its own `paths`, not with the child's narrower list or path scope. The container is the security boundary; the child's `tools` list is the only thing that governs whether the child may send the message. Naming `profile_message` in an agent's tools list therefore hands the child whatever the other profiles in the session can do. The messages are also refused when they would close a wait-for loop between busy profiles (`profile_cycle`).

- **`exec` is an async tool (2026-10-25, user request; same pattern as `tool_smith`).**
  - **Schema.** `exec` gains `block_ms` (integer, 0 or more; a negative value is repaired to 0). `lang`, `code`, `args`, `cwd` and `timeout_ms` are unchanged. `sample/tools/native/exec.json` changed with the Go schema string, so it must be swapped together with the binary.
  - **Behaviour.** The call blocks up to `block_ms` (default and cap from `tools.native.async` `default_block_ms`/`max_block_ms`). A program that ends in time returns its result inline and marks the signal read; one still going returns a handle (`exec_<8 hex>`, kind `exec`, owner = the session key, or the run id for a child that has the async tool) and keeps going on a context of its own, so a cancelled call never kills it. Only `async stop`, a session stop/reset/delete (the manager fan-out), the program's own `timeout_ms` (still clamped to `tools.native.exec` `max_timeout_ms`, itself capped at 6 hours) or server shutdown end it. A handle found running after a restart is `lost`; the program was a child of the old server. The result text is written to `<tool storage>/.async/exec/<id>.txt` (the handle's `ref`) and swept like the `tool_smith` results.
  - **No async tool, no handle.** `tools.native.exec` does **not** need `tools.native.async` (unlike `process`, `subagent` and `tool_smith`, which are refused without it): a session without it, or a call without an owner, runs to its end exactly as before, so existing configurations and the `messages.jsonl` of an inline call do not change.
  - **Code.** The handle plumbing of `tool_smith` moved to `shell_async.go` (`startShellAsync`, one driver per kind, `shellAsyncSpec`); `tool_smith` and `exec` are two specs of it. Behaviour of `tool_smith` is unchanged.
  - **The run-end sweep reaches a handle (2026-11-01, phase 6b, P6BM-04).** A handle used to run on `context.Background()` plus the output watch, so the run's child groups never reached it and `kill_children_on_run_end` silently stopped applying to `exec` and `tool_smith` handles; it now runs on `context.WithoutCancel(ctx)` — the values of the call, none of its cancellation — and a group its program leaves behind is registered and swept at the run's end like an inline call's. Test: `TestAsyncRunKeepsTheChildGroupsOfItsRun`. `exec`'s languages and input shape are unchanged. Nothing was run on Windows.

- **`exec` takes JSON input, saved functions and `argv` (2026-10-25, user request, experimental; `tool_smith` stays JS/TS only and unchanged).** Design: `design_exec.md`. This supersedes the "Interpreter table", "Code delivery" and "Tolerant names" bullets of the `exec` section above.
  - **One input, three modes.** `code` (with `lang`), `function` (a saved one) or `argv` (a program run directly, no shell); none of them, with or without `filters`, lists the saved functions. Giving two names both ("give either code or function, not both"); `lang` with `function` or `argv`, `args` with `argv`, and `filters` with a run are refused naming the keys. The schema is flat and requires nothing, so the executor, not the schema, names what is missing. The near-miss names (`language`, `interpreter`, `source`, `script`, `program`, `command`, `stdin`, `workdir`, `dir`) are no longer rewritten: the schema refuses them with the right key (`RejectedProperties`). One way to say each thing is the lesson of `tool_smith`'s `run`/`function` confusion.
  - **Input is JSON on stdin.** `input` (any JSON value) is written to the program's stdin as one line; without it stdin is empty. Code therefore cannot travel on stdin: `tools.native.exec.langs` became `runners` (the old key is refused with a message naming the new one) and `code` became `mode`: `arg` (default; `{code}` in argv, e.g. `python3 -c {code}`; bash and sh get `exec` as `$0` so `args` start at `$1`), `file` (`{file}`, a 0600 file in the call's scratch directory) or `stdin` (legacy; refused for a call with `input`, naming `runners.<name>.mode`). New keys with defaults: `input_max_bytes` 1 MiB, `arg_max_bytes` 100000 (a single argument is limited to 128 KiB on Linux; larger code is refused, never cut), `functions_dir` `.jad/exec-functions`, `list_max_results` 50, `list_max_files` 200, `list_header_bytes` 2048, `function_max_bytes` 65536. Code containing NUL is refused in mode `arg`. `args` stays beside `input` (compatibility); `input` is the way to pass data.
  - **Result file is `$JAD_RESULT`.** A clean break from `$EXEC_RESULT` (not set any more, no dual name). It is shown ahead of the output and capped by `result_max_bytes`, not parsed. `tool_smith`'s prelude keeps writing `EXEC_RESULT` internally (`toolSmithResultEnv`).
  - **Saved functions.** One file per function, `<name><ext>` in `functions_dir` (its own folder, not `tool_smith`'s), name `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`. The runner is the first one in table order that declares the extension (`.sh` is bash's). The description is the first line of the leading comment block (`#`, `//` or `--`, after a shebang); an optional later `input:` line is shown in the listing. No manifest, so description and code cannot drift; no header overrides of runner or timeout. Listing, filters (`*`/`?` wildcard else substring, every filter must match name or description) and truncation reuse `tool_smith`'s helpers and shape; a malformed file is listed under "malformed files". A call reads the file once from a handle opened without following links, with the same containment checks (inside `functions_dir` and the workspace, regular file, size cap), and runs that text, so an edit during a run changes nothing. An unknown name lists up to 5 similar names; two files with one name are refused.
  - **Handles and cleanup.** All modes go through the same `startShellAsync` handle as before. The scratch directory (`exec-*`, 0700) is removed on every exit. The stdin writer is the existing one-goroutine pipe writer of the child runner, freed when the child ends.
  - **Stale scratch sweep.** The first time `exec` or `tool_smith` is built in a process, private (0700) directories named `exec-*` or `tool-smith-*` in the temp directory and untouched for 7 hours are removed (a call is bounded by the 6-hour ceiling, so none of that age is live). No config key. Links, files and other modes are left alone. It does not cover a server that shares its temp directory with another live server run for over 7 hours on the same call, which the ceiling excludes.
  - **Breaking, experimental:** `sample/tools/native/exec.json` must be swapped with the binary; configs with `langs` fail to load; `EXEC_RESULT` and the old key names stop working.
  - **Not done.** No tool bridge from scripts. No Windows run. `go`/`rust`/`java` runners are not defaults (a toolchain cannot be assumed); add them under `runners` with mode `file`. Edges: a saved function cannot choose a runner other than by extension.
- **`exec` saved functions are helpers that code calls; `function`, `argv`, `args` and the per-call `timeout_ms` are removed (2026-10-25, user decision, experimental; supersedes the "Saved functions" bullet and the "one input, three modes" bullet above).** Design: `design_exec.md`.
  - **Input is flat.** `lang` (default `bash`), `code`, `input` (JSON on stdin, the only way to pass data), `cwd`, `block_ms`; with no `code` the call lists the helpers (`filters` optional). A plain command is `lang` bash with the command as `code`. The old keys fail with the replacement named: `function` ("saved helpers are called from code"), `argv` ("use code with lang"), `args` ("pass data as input"), `timeout_ms` ("the timeout is the configured tools.native.exec.timeout_ms").
  - **Timeout.** Only the per-call parameter went. `tools.native.exec.timeout_ms` (default) stays and is capped at 6 hours like every tool timeout, so a run cannot hang for ever; `max_timeout_ms` is still an accepted key of the shared block but nothing can ask for more than the default any more, so it has no effect on `exec`. A run that outlasts the default is ended as timed out, handle or not.
  - **Layout.** `<functions_dir>/<runner>/<file>` (default `.jad/exec`, never `tool_smith`'s folder). The folder name is a runner name (not an alias) from the `runners` table and selects the language, so the earlier extension-ownership rule for `.sh` is gone. A helper is any regular file whose name matches `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_.-]*$`; dotfiles are skipped; sub-folders stay usable by the language (a Python package). The same name may exist in several languages.
  - **No template, no bridge, no callback.** Nothing is injected around the code. A runner's `lib_env` names the list variable that receives `functions_dir/<runner>` in front of its current value: bash and sh `PATH`, python `PYTHONPATH`, node `NODE_PATH`, ruby `RUBYLIB`, perl `PERL5LIB`; empty turns it off, and a configured runner such as `ts` sets its own. Code then calls a helper the way the language does: a command on `PATH` (the file needs a shebang and the executable bit), `import`, `require("name.js")` (CommonJS only: ESM `import` ignores `NODE_PATH`), `require`, `use`. `JAD_FUNCTIONS_DIR` is the real library root, so a helper reaches another language by a plain subprocess of `$JAD_FUNCTIONS_DIR/<runner>/<file>`. Helpers call helpers the same ways because children inherit the environment. The variable is set only when the library resolves inside the workspace and the runner's folder is a real folder inside it.
  - **`ts` runner (bun) and `node` stay CommonJS-only.** The built-in table gains `ts`: `bun --no-install -e {code}`, `.ts`, aliases `typescript` and `bun`, `lib_env` `NODE_PATH`. bun resolves ESM `import { x } from "read"` (and `"read.ts"`) through `NODE_PATH`, in `-e` and file mode, and helpers import helpers the same way; a tsconfig `baseUrl` did not work for `-e`. `--no-install` stops bun fetching an unknown bare specifier from the npm registry. `node` stays CommonJS-only (`require("read.js")`) because ESM `import` ignores `NODE_PATH` there and fixing it would need a loader or generated file; ESM helpers belong to `ts`. A host without `bun` gets the usual "not found" error for `ts` only.
  - **Template for `fn` (2026-10-26, user decision; supersedes "No template" and the `ts`/`node` bullets above where they differ).** python, node, ts and ruby runners have an optional `template` (`js`, `python`, `ruby`): an embedded script, written per call into the scratch directory and attached with the interpreter's own preload (node `-r {template}`, bun `--preload {template}`, ruby `-r {template}`; python: a `sitecustomize.py` directory in front of `PYTHONPATH`) so user code and its line numbers stay as written. It installs a global `fn`; `fn.<name>` resolves `functions_dir/<runner>/<name>` when read (a helper written by the same program works) and a missing one fails with `no helper fn.<name> in <folder>`. js uses sync `require` (no top-level `await` in a helper; its functions may be async), python and ruby use identifier-like names, `fn["my-helper"]` / `getattr(fn, "my-helper")` for a dash; path-like names are refused. Python runs `importlib.invalidate_caches()` first and then runs any `sitecustomize` ours shadows. `functions_dir/<runner>/_template<ext>` replaces the built-in text (regular file inside the library, else `template_failed`) and is not listed. bash and sh have none (PATH is already looked up per command; `BASH_ENV` would leak into children). `lib_env`, `JAD_FUNCTIONS_DIR`, stdin JSON, `$JAD_RESULT`, exit codes and handles are unchanged; `JAD_RUNNER_DIR` is new. Still no native bridge, perl has no template, cross-language calls are subprocesses. Config: `{template}` exactly once in argv for js/ruby, never for python, refused without a template. Validation findings are in `design_exec.md` section 4.
  - **Limits stated.** There is no depth counter: a cycle A to B to A ends at the one timeout. Stop, reset, delete and restart kill the child's process group (`kill_process_group`), so helpers that stay in it die with it (tested); one that calls `setsid` escapes, as with `bash`. Only the top process's output is captured (nested stdout is part of it), and one `$JAD_RESULT` file serves the whole tree. The interpreter follows symlinks itself, so containment is checked for the listing and for the folder that goes on the path, not for each import. The container is the boundary.
  - **Listing.** Grouped by runner in table order, `name — description (input: ...)`, same limits and filters as `tool_smith`. Entries outside a runner folder, folders that are not runners, bad names, missing descriptions, oversize files and symlinks out of the library are listed as malformed.
  - **Code.** `planExecFunction`, `planExecArgv`, `loadExecFunction`, extension ownership and the `args` handling are deleted; `shellChildRun` gained `prependEnv`.
  - **Listing shows the call, `fn.<name>` is callable (2026-10-27, user decision).** The exec listing prints the expression to type (`fn.read(...)   read.ts — description`; `fn["my-helper"](...)` for js, `getattr(fn, "my-helper")(...)` for python, `fn.public_send("my-helper", ...)` for ruby, the file name for bash/sh) under a legend that states the rule; filters still match the file name and description. The value of `fn.<name>` is both the module and callable: ts/node call the default export, else the export named like the file (`-`/`.` as `_` or camelCase), else the only function; python the function named like the module, else the only public function defined in it; ruby `self.call`, else `self.<name>`, else the only singleton method, and `fn.<name>` without arguments is still the constant. No match is an error naming the exports. Every export stays a property, so `fn.x.member(...)` is unchanged. Sync `require` and the name rules are unchanged; a `_template` override written earlier keeps its old behaviour. design_exec.md no longer says there is no template.
  - **Breaking.** `sample/tools/native/exec.json` changes with the binary; `.jad/exec-functions` is no longer read.


## Native transcript tool (2026-10-27)

- **Single `path` input (rework).** `profile`, `session`, `subagent` and `list` are gone; the old keys fail naming `path`. `path` is `profile:<category>/<name>` (current session), `profile:<session>/<category>/<name>`, `subagent:<run id>`, `subagent:<category>/<name>/<run id>`, `subagent:<session>/<category>/<name>/<run id>` or `path:<file>` (still off unless `allow_path`); no `path` reads the own transcript, and for a sub-agent its own run, found from `ExecutionContext.ProcessOwner` (the run id the manager sets for a child). An unknown prefix, empty segment, `.`/`..`, backslash or NUL is refused with the list of forms. Not found lists the nearest names (`suggest_max`, default 20, replaces `list_max_entries`); the read header prints the exact `path` to reuse; a cursor carries it and is re-checked on every page.

- **Sub-agent grammar follows the layout.** A run lives inside the profile that started it (`<profile tool storage>/.subagents/<call-id>-<hex>/messages.jsonl`, manifest in `.runs/`), so `subagent:<session>/<run id>` is gone and fails with `invalid_path` naming the new forms. `subagent:<run id>` is a run of the caller's profile; `subagent:<category>/<name>/<run id>` and `subagent:<session>/<category>/<name>/<run id>` are runs of another profile, found through the same profile lookup as `profile:` and then the profile's live manager or its manifest on disk. Access switches are unchanged.
- **`transcript` in INFORMATION.** `subagent` (all outcomes with a transcript folder), `profile_message` (target file; the caller's own as `caller_transcript`) and `transcript` (the file read) report the full path of the relevant messages.jsonl under the key `transcript`. It is shown regardless of what the caller may read: the container is the boundary, a path grants nothing, and `transcript`/`read` keep their own path rules. A `profile_message` handle's async `ref` is the target file, as a sub-agent handle's is.
- **Phase 5.** The header reads `turns A-B; the transcript holds turns C-D` (turn numbers, not a count) and adds that the transcript was compacted when C is above 1. Checked by hand against six real transcripts of 18 KB to 1.5 MB: 0.6 to 16 ms a call, output within `response_max_bytes`. Not built: `list` of other sessions and profiles, the `running` flag, a line index cache; (`list` was later removed.)
- **Phase 4.** Selectors `profile` (with `session`), `subagent` and `path` are exclusive (two given: the error names both; `session` alone names `profile`; a cursor already names its transcript and takes none). `profile` goes through the profile messenger's `Profile` lookup (`ProfileMessagesPath` plus the loaded backup path; it loads no run and creates no file). `subagent` resolves the run id with `SubagentManager.RunTranscript` (folder and owning session key); a run of another session needs `allow_other_sessions`, one the caller did not start needs `allow_children`. `path` needs `allow_path` and the read path rules, with symlinks resolved and a transcript check on the first line. A transcript of another profile or session also has to pass the read path rules and the call's path scope (they are checked on the real file, outside the workspace). A cursor carries its selector, so every page is checked again with the current switches. `source: backup` does not apply to `subagent` or `path`.
- **Phase 3.** Filters `role` (user, assistant, tool), `tool` (glob on the tool name; a result is tied to its call by `call_id`), `search` (case-insensitive, with `context` entries around each hit; it matches only entries that pass the other filters) and `source: "backup"` for `messages.backup.jsonl`. A cursor remembers its source, and a different `source` with a cursor is an error.
- **Phases 1-2.** `tools.native.transcript` (off unless the key is present; needs `transcript` in `tool_definition_paths`) reads the caller's own `messages.jsonl` by turn (`turns` N, N-M, -N, N-; `tail`; default the last turn) as text, with a `cursor` to continue a cut response. Design in `design_transcript_reader.md`. Turns are numbered by `session_turn`, so a compacted transcript starts above 1 and the header says so. A cursor holds a hash of the first line and the byte range; a compaction or reset changes the hash and the call fails with `cursor_stale`. Each entry is cut to `entry_max_bytes` (head and tail kept), the response to `response_max_bytes`, a line over `line_max_bytes` is skipped and reported, and a transcript over `scan_max_bytes` is refused. (Output and `entry_max_bytes` are superseded by the v2 section below.)
- **Selectors.** Exactly one of `profile` (with optional `session`), `subagent` or `path`. `path` is off unless `allow_path` is true and follows the read tool's path rules. `subagent` resolves the run id through the sub-agent manager: the subagent result carries the transcript path only for a stopped, timed-out or lost run (and a handle's end), so the id, which the manager always knows, is the reliable key.
- **Settings.** Unknown or old keys are rejected naming the key; `timeout_ms` is capped at 6 hours; the tool has no `paths` key (it reuses `tools.native.read.paths`, like `read_image`). Foreign sessions, profiles and children are readable by default; `allow_other_sessions`, `allow_other_profiles`, `allow_children` turn each off.
- **Read-only scanner (later phase).** The reader opens the file read-only and never uses `Journal.ReadRecordsRange`, which opens the transcript read-write and truncates an incomplete last line.

## `provider_network`: the address family of provider connections (Go only, 2026-10-25)

A host whose direct IPv6 route to the provider is reset mid-stream (a peer reset while reading) while IPv4 works could not be helped: providers used `http.DefaultClient`, and Go only falls back to the other family when a connect fails, not when a read does. HTTP/2 was ruled out as the cause by the report, so there is no HTTP-version option.

- **`provider_network.network` (session setting, also in `defaults`, `settings` of a session or profile).** `"tcp"` (default, either family, nothing changes), `"tcp4"` or `"tcp6"`. An absent key takes the default; a null, non-string, unknown value or unknown key is a configuration error naming the allowed values or the key.
- **Unset changes nothing.** With `tcp` and no OAuth or HTTP debug the provider client stays `nil`, so the SDK keeps `http.DefaultClient`.
- **One base transport.** For `tcp4`/`tcp6` `http.DefaultTransport` is cloned once (proxy from the environment, TLS and `ForceAttemptHTTP2` stay) and only `DialContext` is replaced, with the default dialer's 30s timeout and keep-alive. The OAuth wrapper and the HTTP debug wrapper both sit on that one transport. With an HTTP(S) proxy the family applies to the dial to the proxy, because that is the connection the transport opens.
- **Errors.** A failed dial reads `provider_network.network is "tcp6": <cause>`, so `tcp6` on a host without IPv6 points at the setting.
- **The runner identity includes the setting**, so a changed value builds a new client.
- **Not retried over the other family.** A request that failed after it was sent could have been started by the provider; retrying elsewhere risks a duplicate generation and bill. The user picks the family.
- **Not covered:** the anthropic adapter (a separate service with its own config), the `fetch` tool (pins addresses for SSRF safety), MCP, `decide`, and the OAuth token refresh clients.

## A toolset-based agent may list `subagent` and `profile_message` (Go only, 2026-10-25)

An agent that names a `toolset` could not list `profile_message`: the child pool was built from the toolset's tools only, and the toolset (which must not set `profile_message`) never has it, so loading failed with "the session does not enable tools.native.profile_message". `subagent` already worked, because it is added to the agent's catalog by name. Now the session's own `profile_message` tool is added to the pool of an agent with a toolset, so `tools: ["grep","subagent","profile_message"]` loads and gets all three. A toolset file that sets `tools.native.profile_message` is still a configuration error; the agent's `tools` list still alone decides whether it gets the tool; a session without `profile_message` still fails to load for an agent that lists it. Nesting limits and narrowing are unchanged.

## Native transcript tool v2: a step index and step detail (Go only, 2026-10-28)

Design in `design_transcript_v2.md`. Reading a transcript cost one entry per record, each up to 4 KB, so finding one step meant paying for all of them. v2 has two modes, chosen by `step`.

- **List (no `step`).** One line per step, `turn.step` (`3.2`). A turn starts at a user message (`N.0`); every later assistant message, tool call and reasoning item takes the next number in file order. A tool call and its result are one step. Numbers are the same whatever `turns`, filters or cursor select, and compaction does not renumber (turn numbers still come from `session_turn`). A user or assistant line is its text cut to `preview_bytes`; a call line is `call <tool> <ok|error|unknown|no-result> <result bytes> <args>` with each argument value cut to `value_bytes` and the keys whole; a result is not a line (an orphan result is); reasoning is listed nowhere, so numbers have gaps where it was. Filters `role`, `tool`, `search`, `context` stay; search tests the full text, including a call's arguments and result, and prints only the preview.
- **Detail (`step`).** `3.2` or a range `3.1-3.4` (may cross turns), `part` call, result, both (default) or reasoning (never shown unless asked). The text of the steps is one stream paged by byte offset in `max_detail_bytes` (and never above `response_max_bytes`) on rune boundaries; the cursor carries the step range, part, offset and file identity. Not with `turns`, `role`, `tool`, `search`, `context`; `full` is rejected.
- **Order (amended 2026-10-28).** The list is newest step first, in descending file order across turns (17.100, 17.99, ... 17.0, 16.40, ...); step numbers are unchanged. Without `turns` the whole transcript is listed, page by page; `turns` limits it and the order stays newest first. A list cursor names the newest step still unlisted and fixes the byte range chosen by the first call, so it continues toward older steps, and steps appended between pages are never shown by it nor shift or repeat older pages (the identity check is unchanged). `tail` is removed rather than kept as 'newest N': the first page already is the newest steps and `turns -N` selects the last N turns; `tail` is rejected with that hint. A detail range (`3.1-3.4`) stays ascending inside its one response, since it reads one passage.
- **Middle cut.** `midCut` keeps 70% head and 30% tail on rune boundaries with `...+N bytes...` in the middle, total within the budget; a budget too small for the marker returns the bare marker, or the text if that is shorter.
- **Settings.** New `preview_bytes` (200), `value_bytes` (80), `max_list_entries` (100), `max_detail_bytes` (16384), capped. `entry_max_bytes` and `max_turns_per_call` are removed and rejected naming the key.
- **Unchanged.** `path` grammar, access switches, the read-only scanner, `source: backup`, symlink and path-rule checks, cursor identity; the strings `transcripts` prints still work.
- **Known limit.** A detail cursor is a byte offset into text rebuilt for each page; a call that gets its result between two pages (the file only grew) can shift the stream.
- **Measured** on the 323 messages.jsonl files of `.jad/state` that are over 20 KB (57.7 MB): listing every turn took 22.0 MB of output with the old tool (entries cut at 4 KB) and 1.4 MB with the list mode, 6.4% of it.

## Native transcripts tool (Go only, 2026-10-26)

`tools.native.transcripts` lists what `transcript` can read; design in `design_transcripts.md`. It is separate from `transcript` (which dropped `list` on purpose), off unless the key is present, and needs `transcripts` in `tool_definition_paths` (`sample/tools/native/transcripts.json`).

- **Input.** `path` (none: the profiles of the caller's session; `sessions`; `session:<id>`; `profile:<category>/<name>` or `profile:<session>/<category>/<name>`: that profile's sub-agent runs), `filters` (the `tool_smith` rules: substring, or `*`/`?` wildcard, all must match the name; for runs also the agent name and parent call), `status` (runs only), `limit`, `cursor`. An unknown key fails schema validation naming it.
- **Output.** Plain text, newest first by last activity (mtime of `messages.jsonl`, for a run its end or start when it has none), one line per item with the exact `path` to give `transcript` and the full file path, size and time, and for profiles whether `messages.backup.jsonl` exists. No message counts: that would scan every file. The legend line is printed only when there are items. A missing directory lists as empty, never an error.
- **Paging.** The cursor is keyset (last activity and key), so a profile created or a file touched between pages never repeats or drops an unrelated item. It carries the level, filters and status and is checked again against the current switches.
- **Access.** Own switches with the meaning they have for `transcript`: `allow_other_sessions`, `allow_other_profiles`, `allow_children`, all default true, so `transcripts` can be on while `transcript` is off or stricter; a listed path may then still be refused by `transcript`. With the switches off only the caller's own profile and its runs are listed. A run is listed under the rule `transcript` applies to reading it. A file the read path rules deny (`tools.native.read.paths`, narrowed by a sub-agent's scope) is left out and counted under `hidden`.
- **Read-only.** It stats files and reads run manifests (`.runs/<id>.json`); it never opens a transcript and never uses the repairing `Journal` reader. A `messages.jsonl` that is a symlink is followed only while its target stays in its own folder, and a run folder must stay inside the sub-agent storage directory, else the item is listed as `unreadable` with no size.
- **Runs.** Manifests on disk and the live manager are merged (the live state wins). With `persist_runs` off the header says finished runs have no manifest and only runs the running server holds are listed. A profile without the subagent tool says it has no sub-agent storage.
- **Caps.** `max_entries` (default 50, cap 500), `scan_max_entries` (2000, cap 50000; more candidates fail with `too_many` and ask for filters), `timeout_ms` (30 s, cap 6 h). `max_entries` may not exceed `scan_max_entries`.
- **Code.** `TranscriptTarget` gained `PersistRuns`; `profileMessenger` gained `Sessions`; `SubagentManager.allRuns` lists every retained run. `transcript` itself is unchanged.
- **Not covered.** Non-catalog sessions (the profile source refuses them), the `path:<file>` kind, and message counts.

## Remote JAD calling (Go only, 2026-10-29)

Design: `design_remote.md`. A JAD can act as a client of another JAD (`profile_message`, `subagent`, `transcript`, `transcripts`) and, separately, serve `/api/remote/v1` to others. Commits: entry loader, client transport, server API, `profile_message`, `subagent`, transcripts, docs.

- **Remote entry.** `<remotes folder>/<name>.json`, `kind: "remote"`, strict keys (`remote.schema.json`), in a sibling `remotes/` catalog folder, not among profiles or agents. It holds `url` (`http://host:port` or `unix:///path`), `token_env` (must match `JAD_REMOTE_*`) or `token_file`, caps, `default_session` and a `profiles` allow-list. A token or `proxy` key is refused by name, and so is every TLS key of the https era (`ca_file`, `insecure`, `insecure_skip_verify`, `skip_verify`, `tls`), each with the reason. The file is read on every call, so an edit applies at the next call.
- **Transport.** Own `http.Transport` per entry: plain http, no proxy from the environment, no redirect followed (a 3xx is an error), response read through a `max_response_bytes` limit, per-request timeout, bounded in-flight requests, retries only for reads and for starts that carry a `request_id`. Errors name the remote and a short cause; they never contain the token, the URL or the query (a `*url.Error` is unwrapped; regression test `TestNetworkTextHidesTheRequestURL`).
- **Server.** `server.remote_api` (off by default) serves the routes on exactly one listener: the unix socket (mode 0600 by default) when `listen_unix` is set, never the network, or the main listener, which is plain http, when it is empty. Tokens are rows of a JSON file holding the SHA-256 of the secret, `expires`, `revoked`, globs for `profiles` (`session/category/profile`) and `agents` (`.../agent`), `actions` (`message`, `subagent`, `status`, `stop`, `transcripts`, `profile`) and `max_hops`. The `profile` action lets a token read a profile's info (its context tokens) through `GET /api/remote/v1/profile?session=&profile=`; compaction and reset are never served remotely, and a profile outside the token's globs answers like one that does not exist. The file is re-read when it changes, so a revocation is immediate. Runs belong to the token that started them. A start carries a `request_id`; a repeat returns the same run, never a second message. Only sessions that exist are addressed.
- **Loop guard.** `X-Jad-Hops` is sent as the caller's count plus one; the server refuses past `min(token.max_hops, server max_hops)` (default 3) with 508-style `remote_hop_limit`. A remote may call back into the first JAD, bounded by hops.
- **Async.** A remote message or sub-agent is a handle of the existing async tool; `wait`, `status` and `stop` act on the remote run. Following is by polling with `wait_ms`, no held stream. A remote unreachable for `lost_after_ms` ends the handle as `lost` ("may still be running there"). After a client restart handles end like local ones; they are not rebuilt. A local timeout stops the remote run.
- **Targets.** `profile_message`: `remote:<name>/<session>/<category>/<profile>`. `subagent` agent: `remote:<name>/<session>/<category>/<profile>/<agent>`. The session may be left out when the entry has a `default_session`. Each tool lists the usable remotes in `tools.native.<tool>.remotes` (none by default; checked at load), so a session reaches no other server unless it says so.
- **Transcripts.** `remote:<name>/` is a prefix in front of the existing `kind:` grammar: `remote:<name>/profile:<session>/<category>/<name>`, `remote:<name>/subagent:<session>/<category>/<name>/<run id>`, and for `transcripts` `remote:<name>/session:<id>` (`remote:<name>` alone lists the default session). `path:` is never accepted after `remote:`. The remote runs its real `transcript`/`transcripts` code (step numbers, truncation, list and detail modes, cursors) and returns the rendered text, so little crosses the wire and there is one implementation of the format. The client only forwards a fixed set of keys and prefixes the `path` fields and the cursor with `remote:<name>/`, which keeps every emitted string copy-pasteable. A cursor given back (`remote:<name>/<cursor>`) is a remote read and obeys the same switch; the remote ties it to file identity as for a local read.
- **Own switch.** `tools.native.transcript.allow_remote` and `tools.native.transcripts.allow_remote` (default false) with `remotes` (required with it, refused without it). The remote's token must also have the `transcripts` action; its globs decide the profiles. The remote API builds the tools with `HidePaths`: no `transcript:` information key and no `| file` field, so no path of the remote machine leaves it. A profile outside the token's globs answers like one that does not exist.
- **Not done / limits.** The server's `/api/remote/v1/transcript(s)` need an explicit session (no "own" transcript for a caller); the remote does not read `path:`; a client cannot list a remote's sub-agent runs of a profile that has persist_runs off beyond what that server holds (same as local). Real network, Windows and a token table on a networked filesystem are not tested. The token table needs a restart to turn the API on; token edits do not.
- **Remote API run caps (review fix).** `server.remote_api.max_runs_per_token` (default 16, 1 to 10000) bounds the runs one token has going; finished runs never count against it. `max_runs` still bounds all held runs, but a full table drops the oldest finished run (the asking token's own first) instead of refusing, so a token cannot lock the others out for `run_retention_ms`. A start is refused (429 `too_many_runs`) only when the token is at its live cap or every held run is live.
- **Remote API token table and globs (review fix).** The table file must not be accessible by group or others (like client token files); a table that loses 0600 after start refuses every request until fixed. It is re-read when its SHA-256 or mode changes (not size+mtime, which a same-size rewrite within the timestamp granularity defeats), looked at most every 250 ms under a read-write lock. Globs in token `profiles`/`agents` and in a remote entry's `profiles` may not contain `[`: a character class can match `/`, which would break "`*` never crosses `/`".
- **Plain http only, HTTPS removed (2026-10-30, user decision).** The user runs local -> VPN -> local and asked for the simpler thing: remote calling is `http://host[:port]` or `unix:///absolute/path.sock`, and there is no TLS anywhere in it. An `https://` url is refused with its own message ("https was removed ..."), the client sets no `TLSClientConfig`, and the server's `tls_cert_file`/`tls_key_file` are gone: both are refused by name with the reason, never ignored, and the main listener is never wrapped in TLS. `/api/remote/v1` therefore answers on the plain listener, or only on the unix socket when `listen_unix` is set, so a socket keeps them off the network; `server.remote_api.enabled` needs only `tokens_file` now. Everything else stays as it was (token table, `token_env`/`token_file`, hop guard, `request_id`, no redirects, no proxy, caps, token scrubbing, socket owner and directory checks). **The bearer token now travels in clear text over tcp:** that connection must be a VPN or a private network, a unix socket is the local option, and the warning is in `design_remote.md`, `remote.schema.json`, `root-setting.schema.json` and `sample/jad/remotes/build-box.json`. **Listen address:** a wildcard is refused when the routes are on TCP — with `server.remote_api` enabled and no `listen_unix`, `server.host`/`HOST` must name one address (`app.LoadConfig`, which refuses a wildcard host), the default `127.0.0.1` included in that rule; a peer on the VPN reaches this server only when the operator writes that address, so exposing the token-taking routes is deliberate. With `listen_unix` set the routes are on the socket alone and the host rule does not apply. No new listener and no new setting: the routes use the main listener or the socket.
- **Remote API: hop count and sub-agent transcripts (review fix).** `X-Jad-Hops` is supplied by the caller and trusted: it bounds cooperating JAD peers against loops, not a hostile token holder (who can send 0 every time); access is bounded by the token's actions and globs and by `max_runs_per_token`. Reading a sub-agent run's transcript, or listing a profile's runs, through `/transcript(s)` also requires the token's `agents` glob for `session/category/profile/agent`; a run outside it answers exactly like a missing run (same text, no `available` hint naming it). Profile transcripts are still scoped by `profiles` only.

- **exec accepts `command` as an alias of `code` (2026-10-30).** Models that send the bash/process name get a run instead of a refusal. `code` stays the only documented field (schema, description and `sample/tools/native/exec.json` are unchanged); `normalizeExecArguments` folds `command` into `code` before validation, so the permission, hook, checkpoint and `tool_smith` bridge paths, which all normalize first, see one field. Equal values are fine; different values (or a non-string `command`) stay as `command` and the schema refuses them naming both keys, so one is never picked silently. `lang` default is unchanged. This supersedes the `command` hint in the entry above.

- **Async "latest" block (2026-10-31, user decision).** When a start tool (`process`, `exec`, `tool_smith` run, `profile_message`, `subagent`) returns because `block_ms` ran out, or the `async` tool's `wait` returns because `wait_ms` ran out, `list` or `status` is called, every handle that is still live gets a short `latest` entry showing only what is new since the last time that handle's progress was shown. Nothing about timeouts or kills changes: the work keeps running. One shared builder (`tools/native/async_latest.go`) asks the handle's driver (`asyncLatester`); the cursor is kept per handle in the manager (`SetProgress`/`Progress`, memory only, dropped when the handle ends), so each return moves it. Output kinds show the new stdout and stderr separately, each cut at the front to `latest_bytes` with `…+N bytes earlier`, plus the age and the time since the last output; with nothing new the block says `no new output since <time>`. Output is read from the live collector (stdout and stderr windows, now guarded by a mutex), not from files. Agent kinds (`subagent`, `profile_message`) show the last `latest_lines` tool calls (name and arguments with values cut to `latest_value_bytes`) and assistant replies as one-line previews, the age of the last transcript write, the transcript path and the last step number for the `transcript` tool; reasoning, tool results and user text are never shown, and a transcript larger than `latest_scan_max_bytes` shows only its path. The cursor of an agent is a transcript offset; `profile_message` starts it at the transcript's end when the message starts, so earlier turns of a long-lived target are not replayed. A remote run (`remote_subagent`, a remote `profile_message`) shows only its run id and age, no paths. One `wait`/`list`/`status` response adds at most `latest_total_bytes` of blocks; a handle that no longer fits keeps its cursor and is shown by the next call. Finished handles are returned exactly as before. New `tools.native.async` keys (validated, schema updated): `latest_bytes` 500, `latest_lines` 3, `latest_total_bytes` 2000, `latest_value_bytes` 80, `latest_scan_max_bytes` 8388608. Not done: a flag to re-show the full tail, and a block for the queued state beyond "waiting for its turn".

- **MCP discovery follows pages and is bounded (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `tools/list` was read from one response, so a server whose page size is smaller than its catalog showed only its first page and said nothing (`ServerOptions{PageSize: 2}` with five tools loaded two, `err == nil`). `collectToolPages` now follows `NextCursor` inside the existing `listCtx` (one `timeout_ms` for the whole walk), keeps the server's order, and stops at the first error as before. The page count is bounded by the tool budget instead of a number of its own: at most `max_tools+1` requests: a server that returns at least one tool per page hits the tool bound first, and a server that answers a fresh cursor with no tools still stops; a cursor that repeats is refused on the page it would repeat. Three new keys of an `mcp/<server>.json` file (validated like `max_result_bytes`, schema `sample/schemes/mcp-server.schema.json`, in the normalized fingerprint so a change reconnects):

  | key | default | min | max | what it bounds |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `max_tools` | 256 | 1 | 2048 | tools a server may expose, and through `max_tools+1` the `tools/list` requests |
  | `max_schema_bytes` | 65536 | 512 | 1048576 | one tool's `inputSchema` JSON bytes |
  | `max_description_bytes` | 4096 | 1 | 65536 | one tool's description (it is sent with every request) |

  Defaults come from the phase-6a measurement, not from taste: 500 tools with 16 KiB schemas cost 158 ms of CPU and 229 MB of allocation per run (about 458 KB per tool, 28x its schema, because each schema is copied, marshalled, compiled, cached and re-serialized into every request). 256 tools and a 64 KiB schema still load a large ordinary server, while the same 500-tool server is refused by count or by schema size instead of costing a quarter of a gigabyte each run; 4 KiB of description keeps the standing prompt cost of a full catalog near 1 MB. **Exceeding a bound fails loudly**: the run stops with `MCP server <id> returned more than max_tools (256) tools`, `... did not finish tools/list within max_tools (256) pages`, `... repeated the tools/list cursor on page N`, `MCP tool <id>/<tool> inputSchema is N bytes, above max_schema_bytes (65536)` or `... description is N bytes, above max_description_bytes (4096)`. Tools are never dropped silently, which is the difference between this and the first-page bug: the old behaviour was a smaller catalog with no error. Not changed: the schema cache keeps its 512-entry bound (each entry is now at most `max_schema_bytes`), the tool list is still fetched once per catalog build, and there is no list TTL (P-006(b) stays rejected). A file that sets `max_tools: 0`, a key that is not an integer or a value out of range is now a configuration error, so it fails on load rather than at the first run.

- **A rejected call reports a bounded sample of its issues (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `collectValidationIssues` appends one issue per failing element and `ExecuteFunctionCall` put all of them into the result: 1,000 bad items (4,007 bytes of arguments) produced a 162,135-byte `ajv_errors` array, 40.5x the input, which is persisted to `messages.jsonl` and re-sent on every round. The report now keeps the first `maxReportedValidationIssues` (20) issues, cuts the strings inside an issue to `maxReportedValidationTextSize` (200) bytes with the existing `tools.CutText` plus `...`, and appends one final `{"keyword":"","message":"N more issues omitted"}` entry. The per-string cut has its own reason: the instance and schema paths carry property names from the arguments, and an `enum`/`const` keyword carries values from the schema, so one issue can be large on its own. `Validate` still returns every issue — the AJV shape, its params and the existing tests depend on the full list — and only the model-facing report is bounded. Measured after the change: the same call reports 3,066 bytes and says `980 more issues omitted`; a one-issue failure renders exactly as before. Not changed: `SchemaError`'s shape, and the two callers that never show issues to the model (the remote transcript refusal and the tool-definition load error).

- **`decide_systemone` redacts the key before it cuts the provider text (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `requestDecisions` cut the provider's error text to 400 bytes and redacted the key afterwards, so a key that straddled the cut kept a prefix of itself in the reported `reason` (the phase-6a probe found the first 12 of 39 characters). `decideSystemoneErrorMessage` takes the key and redacts first — after `oneLine`, before `cutUTF8(text, 400)` — so the text becomes `...key=[redacte` instead of a key prefix. The 422 `detail` shape goes through the same function, so both provider error shapes are covered by one ordering, and the regression test puts the key across byte 400 in each.

- **MCP structured content is encoded once, and only when it fits `max_result_bytes` (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `orderedJSONObject.MarshalJSON` called `marshalJSON` per field, and `marshalJSON` encodes into a buffer and then copies the whole value again through `unescapeJSONSeparators`, so every nesting level re-encoded and re-copied its whole subtree: cost `depth × size`. Measured here with a 1 MiB leaf: depth 1,000 took **5.89 s and allocated 7.39 GB** (about 7 copies of the document per level), depth 200 took 1.01 s / 1.49 GB, depth 3 took 22.9 ms / 34.7 MB. `appendOrderedJSONValue` now writes keys, scalars, nested objects and arrays into **one** destination buffer, so the cost is linear in the payload: the same depth-1,000 case takes **15.1 ms and allocates 20.4 MB** (390x faster, 362x less memory, 44,479 → 10,131 allocations), and depth 3/50/200/1,000 all land at 13–15 ms and ~20 MB for the same 1 MiB payload. The benchmark is `BenchmarkPreserveStructuredContentOrder` (depths 3/50/200/1,000, `-benchmem`), and `TestPreserveStructuredContentOrderStaysLinearInNestingDepth` asserts the converted value is byte-identical to its input and allocates at most 64 bytes per payload byte (deterministic; wall-clock time is not asserted). **No new setting and no depth constant:** `structuredContentForResult` keeps a payload larger than `max_result_bytes` exactly as the server sent it, because the content parts are cut to that limit anyway and ordering would spend the work on bytes that never reach the model; that same check is the only bound the conversion needs, since a JSON value of n bytes nests at most n/2 levels. A result that fits is ordered (and its duplicate keys collapsed) exactly as before; a result above the limit keeps the server's key order and is cut to the limit, which in practice is the stdio path and any transport that does not bound the frame, because `streamable_http` already refuses a response body above `max_result_bytes` (P6AM-01). Not changed: the SDK's nested-content decode and the other copies in `contentParts`/`marshalParts` (PH3), the schema-cache bound, and the `max_result_bytes` validation.

- **MCP structured content keeps the number literals the server sent (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** Structured content passed through `float64` twice, and the second pass was fatal: `{"m":1e400}` made the whole tool call fail with `preserve MCP structured-content order: ... cannot unmarshal number 1e400 into Go value of type float64`, and `{"id":9007199254740993}` came out as `9007199254740992`. `preserveStructuredContentOrder` now decodes with `json.Decoder.UseNumber` and `appendOrderedJSONNumber` writes the literal, so an integer beyond 2^53 keeps its digits. A literal above float64's range becomes `null`, which is what `JSON.stringify` writes for the `Infinity` that `JSON.parse` produces (underflow keeps the literal, since the digits are still the server's). Two consequences worth naming: the conversion no longer applies JavaScript's shortest-form printing, so `1.0` stays `1.0` and `1e2` stays `1e2` (a data pipeline should not rewrite digits it was given), and the **first** float64 pass was not in this package at all. The MCP SDK decodes `structuredContent` into a generic `any` while unmarshalling the response, so dropping only our own `float64` would still have failed the call at `calling "tools/call": ... structuredContent.m of type float64`. That decode is redundant — `structuredContentCapture` already keeps the exact bytes, and `executeTool` replaces the decoded value with the captured one — so `structuredContentConnection.Read` drops the member (`withoutStructuredContent`) once the capture has it. The SDK therefore no longer builds a second copy of a value the pipeline already holds. Not changed: structured content decoded without the capture is not a path (`newStructuredContentCapture` is created for every connection), and a response that does not carry the field is passed through untouched.

- **A stdio MCP server is stopped as a process group (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `getConnection` built `exec.Command` with no `SysProcAttr`, and the SDK's shutdown closes stdin, sends SIGTERM and then SIGKILL to `cmd.Process` only (`cmd.go`, `pipeRWC.Close`), so a wrapper (`npx`, `uvx`, `sh -c …`) left the real server running as an orphan against a closed stdin, holding its ports and files. `configureStdioProcessGroup` now sets `Setpgid` before the SDK starts the command, so the wrapper and everything it starts share one group, and `connection.close` calls `killStdioProcessGroup` after `session.Close()` returns, which so far always means the leader was reaped by `cmd.Wait` — a process group id stays reserved while the group has a member, so `kill(-pgid)` still names the surviving grandchildren and cannot have been handed to an unrelated process. Order matters and is deliberate: the direct child keeps the SDK's graceful sequence (EOF, SIGTERM, 5 s, SIGKILL), and the group is killed last, so no second grace period is added to a shutdown that P6AM-05 already measures; a member still alive at that point has had the EOF its parent's exit implies. **Unix only by user decision** (`process_group_unix.go` with `Setpgid` and `kill(-pgid, SIGKILL)`; `process_group_other.go`, which covers Windows, keeps both as no-ops, so Windows starts and stops a stdio server exactly as before). No new setting and no new timeout. The regression test `TestManagerStopsStdioGrandchildOnClose` (`//go:build !windows`) starts a fixture MCP server that spawns `sleep 600000`, calls `Manager.Close`, and requires the grandchild's pid to be gone; it fails both with `Setpgid` removed and with the group kill removed. Not changed: `Manager.Close` still waits for an in-flight call and closes connections concurrently, and a connect that fails leaves the group cleanup to the same two calls.

- **`max_result_bytes` cuts the part that does not fit instead of dropping it (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `boundedContent` only truncated a text part when nothing had fitted yet (`len(bounded) == 0`), and sized the kept text as `(budget-notice-128)/6` to allow for the worst case of `\u00XX` expansions, so an `isError` result whose message was over the limit reached the model as the canned marker alone ("MCP server reported a tool error.", 179 bytes of a 2,048-byte budget), a short first part cost the model a large second part whole (148 bytes), and a single 3,000-byte text kept 201 bytes (447 bytes of the budget). Now the first part that does not fit is cut wherever it sits — the marker stays first, the text follows — and `fitTextPart` measures the **encoded** size of the candidate part and shrinks by the measured ratio until it fits, so escaping is paid for once per attempt instead of being assumed away. The same three cases now end at exactly 2,048 bytes each (`parts=3` for the error and the short-then-long cases, `parts=2` for the single text). The result still ends with the omission notice, still cuts on a character boundary, and still falls back to the notice alone when nothing fits. Tests: `TestBoundedContentKeepsTheErrorTextAboveTheLimit`, `TestBoundedContentCutsThePartPastTheBudgetInsteadOfDroppingIt`, `TestBoundedContentUsesMostOfTheBudgetForOneLongText`.

- **One marshaler and one cutter for the bytes the model and `messages.jsonl` see (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** Three "encrypt without HTML escaping" helpers existed (`tools.MarshalJSON`, `mcp.marshalJSON`, `extension.marshalDecideSystemoneJSON`), and the first was not the one the wire used: the `function_call_output` envelope was built with `json.Marshal` of a struct holding a `json.RawMessage`, which compacts the raw value with **HTML escaping on**, so the `\u2028` pass in `mcp` was undone and `max_input_bytes` (documented as counting "the JSON as sent (compact)") counted up to ~6x fewer bytes than `decide_systemone` sent for `<&>`-heavy input. `tools.MarshalJSON` is now the only encoder on that path — `SetEscapeHTML(false)` plus the string-aware `replaceJSONStringSeparators` pass, which is what `JSON.stringify` prints — and `encodeFunctionCallOutputValue`, `encodeFunctionCallOutput`, `DraftJSONBytes`, every MCP result and the `decide_systemone` request body go through it; `mcp.marshalJSON`, `mcp.unescapeJSONSeparators` (the whole-document variant of the separator pass) and `extension.marshalDecideSystemoneJSON` are gone. Three UTF-8 cutters became one: `extension.cutUTF8` and `mcp.truncateUTF8` were deleted in favour of `tools.CutText`, which is also the fix for `CutText(-1)` — it used to index `text[-1]` and panic, and `cutUTF8` had the same shape, so both are now clamped by `maxBytes = max(maxBytes, 0)` and a negative limit simply cuts everything. **Behaviour change worth naming:** the escaping policy now reaches `messages.jsonl`, so a `function_call_output` that contains `<`, `>`, `&`, U+2028 or U+2029 is written unescaped where it used to be written as `\u003c`, `\u0026`, `\u2028`; the value is JSON-equal and every existing test and golden file still matches, but a byte-for-byte diff of an old transcript shows the difference. Tests: `TestFunctionCallOutputEnvelopeKeepsTheEncodedCharacters`, `TestMarshalJSONKeepsAnglesAndSeparators`, `TestCutTextClampsANegativeLimit`, `TestDecideSystemoneRequestKeepsTheJSONInputBytesItCounted`. Not done here: `firstNonEmpty` (four copies) and the parallel helpers in `tools/native` belong to that package's phase.

- **A recovered tool failure leaves a log line, and the panic boundary covers normalization (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `ExecuteFunctionCall` turns a panicking or failing tool into the same canned result (`tool_execution_failed`), and it replaced the error without logging it, so a panicking tool left no trace in any log; `NormalizeArguments` and `Validator.Validate` also ran outside the recover (`executeRecovered`/`executeOutputRecovered` wrapped `Execute`/`ExecuteOutput` only). `logToolFailure` now logs the cause once with the package-level `slog` functions (`tool`, `call_id`, `error`) — the model-facing text is unchanged, and the `tools` package stays free of a logger setting, so no config key was added; like `internal/tools/native` (`process_orphan.go`) the record goes to `slog`'s process default handler, not to the `log.*`-configured logger, because `Catalog` holds no logger and the app sets no default. Normalization runs inside the same boundary (`normalizeRecovered`) and a panicking normalizer returns the canned result plus the log line instead of ending a caller that has no recover of its own (the `app/remote_api.go` sub-agent goroutine); validation runs inside it too (`validateRecovered`), and a panic there becomes the error `ExecuteFunctionCall` already returns. The remote API's sub-agent goroutine had no recover of its own — it was the one place a tool ran with nothing above it — so it now carries a one-line boundary (`callSubagentTool`): a panic there is logged the same way, the call returns no output, and the caller reports the ordinary "the subagent tool did not start a run" refusal. Tests: `TestCatalogLogsTheRecoveredFailure` (an executor error and a panicking `ExecuteOutput` both log, and neither cause reaches the model), `TestCatalogContainsAPanickingNormalizer` (D-1's own requested regression), `TestCallSubagentToolRecoversAPanic` (the remote API goroutine logs the panic, returns no output and still answers with the refusal). Not covered: the panic value is logged without a stack.

- **`decide_systemone` keeps a missing confidence missing and refuses a probability outside 0-1 (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** `decideSystemoneAnswer.Confidence` was a plain `float64`, so an answer the provider sent without `confidence` was indistinguishable from one it sent as `0`, and `decideSystemoneAnswerFits` checked only the type and the presence of the number, so `noul: 1.5` (or `-0.2`) was echoed as a probability and `confidence: 1.5` as a confidence. `Confidence` is now a `*float64`, and `decideSystemoneProbabilityFits` refuses a probability or a confidence outside 0-1 by marking the answer `"status":"unusable"`, like an answer of the wrong type. A score is unchanged: it is a level index that may fall between levels and is clamped to the nearest label, which an existing test pins deliberately. **Output-shape change:** a choice or score answer whose provider reply carried no `confidence` used to print `"confidence":0` and now carries no `confidence` key at all; an answer with `confidence` present is byte-identical to before. Test: `TestDecideSystemoneKeepsAMissingConfidenceMissingAndRefusesAnOutOfRangeOne`.

- **The `decide_systemone` questions share the `max_input_bytes` budget (2026-11-01, phase 6a).** Only the `input` parts were measured; `parseDecideSystemoneQuestions` trimmed the strings and never measured them and put no bound on the option count, so `max_questions` × unlimited instructions, option labels and descriptions went out as one unbounded request — measured in phase 6a at 200 KiB of input costing 3.6 ms / 3.8 MB and 128 questions × 50 options costing 19.4 ms / 11.8 MB with nothing in front of them. One counter now covers the whole request: `max_input_bytes` bounds the input parts plus the questions as they are sent (`systemoneQuestionItems` marshalled with `tools.MarshalJSON`, the same text the request body carries), refused before any request with `input_too_large` and a summary that names the setting, e.g. `The request is 63 bytes (22 input, 41 questions); max_input_bytes is 61`. No key was added: the option-count half is inside the same budget (40 long options cannot fit), so `max_options` is not needed — the 128 × 50 row costs 19.4 ms and 11.8 MB of transient allocation, which is not worth a setting. **Behaviour change:** an input that exactly filled `max_input_bytes` and left no room for the questions is now refused, and the input-only `Input is N bytes; the limit is M` summary became the combined one above. Tests: `TestDecideSystemoneRefusesQuestionsPastTheByteBudget`, and `TestDecideSystemoneInputLimitCountsAllPartsAndTheJSONAsSent` / `TestDecideSystemoneRefusesBadQuestionsBeforeAnyRequest` moved to the combined numbers.

## Phase 6b: the concurrency batch of the native command tools (Go only, 2026-11-01)

Audit: `audit_findings/p6b_merged.md` (batch (i): P6BM-01, 04, 06, 08, 10). P6BM-01 and P6BM-04 are recorded in their own sections (`tool_smith` "Nested context", `exec` "the run-end sweep reaches a handle").

- **A manifest written by a stop never replaces a terminal one (P6BM-08).** `Stop` cloned the manifest under `entry.mu`, released it, then wrote `manager.manifests[id]` and the file, so a process that finished inside that window kept `state: running` for the rest of the server's life: the run-capacity count and `runningOwned` counted a dead process, `prune` never removed a non-terminal manifest, and `Recover` marked the process `lost` after a restart. `setLeftRunning` had the same shape and a wider window. `recordLiveManifest` now writes both places only while the entry is live and is called with `entry.mu` held, and `finish` takes `entry.mu` before it sets the terminal state, so the terminal write is always last. `Close` no longer inspects entries while holding `manager.mu`: the map write takes `entry.mu` first and `manager.mu` second, so the old nesting would deadlock. Test: `TestStopKeepsTheTerminalManifestOfAProcessThatExitsFirst` (the guard, plus 20 stops racing a process that exits at once).
- **A stop that arrives before the process exists is carried out (P6BM-10).** Between `Async.Begin` and `command.Start` the entry is live with no pid and no command, so `Stop` took its "nothing to signal" branch: it set the reason, reported the process left running and returned, and `Start` spawned the child anyway with nothing left to stop it (reachable from a sub-agent run end and from shutdown). `Start` now reads the reason under `entry.mu`, clears the `left_running` flag the early stop had to set, and runs the same SIGTERM-then-SIGKILL sequence `Stop` uses (`stopSignalled`, now shared by both) after the running manifest is persisted. Test: `TestStopBeforeTheProcessStartsStillStopsIt`, which stops the process from inside `Async.Begin` — the exact window.
- **A recycled process group id is told apart from the group a call registered (P6BM-06).** `ChildGroups.add` stored only the group id, and the run-end sweep re-checked liveness — often hours after the call, since a group id stays reserved only while the group has a member. A group that ended in between hands its number back, so the sweep could signal an unrelated process group. Each entry now keeps the leader's start time where the system reports one (`processStartTime`, /proc on Linux, zero elsewhere) and the sweep checks it before SIGTERM and again before the hard kill; with no start time only liveness is checked, as before. Groups observed dead are dropped when a later call registers its own. Neighbouring paths are unchanged and safe for their own reasons (`killGroupAfter`, `endProcessGroup` and the orphan path poll liveness inside one grace window, and the orphan path compares start times). Tests: `TestRunEndSweepSkipsAGroupWhoseIdWasHandedOutAgain` (an entry whose recorded start time is another leader's is left alone), `TestRunEndSweepStopsTheGroupItRegistered` (the positive half), `TestChildGroupsAddRecordsTheLeaderStartTime`. Not reproducible in a test: the real pid wrap.

## Phase 6b: the log default and the latest and close fixes of the native command tools (Go only, 2026-11-01)

Audit: `audit_findings/p6b_merged.md` (batch (iv): P6BM-05, 13, 15, 16).

- **A process log stream is capped at 8 MiB, not 64 MiB, by default (P6BM-13, config round).** At the shipped defaults a tool-storage directory could reach 12.5 GiB of process logs: `tools.native.process.log_cap_bytes` 64 MiB per stream (`native_process_settings.go`), two streams per process (`process_manager.go`, the stdout and stderr spill writers), and `max_completed` 100 finished processes kept with their folders before `prune` removes anything — plus up to `max_running` 8 more running, another 1 GiB. A `process` call is model-reachable and a chatty child (`yes`, a build log) fills its share without any mistake by the user. The default is now 8 MiB per stream, which is 8 MiB x 2 x 100 = 1.6 GiB per storage directory, and at most 8 x 16 MiB = 128 MiB live. No key was added: the knob exists and is per session. **Parity check first, as decision 5 asked:** the 64 MiB value came from the TypeScript backend's process settings (commits `e12e430`/`74d2c11`); the TypeScript backend, the differential harness and the `config-parity` goldens are gone (decided 2026-10-10), and no remaining golden or case file carries a process default (`grep log_cap internal/sessionfs/testdata/` finds nothing), so nothing verifies the old number and this is a Go-only default change. The three places that published it — the loader, `sample/schemes/setting.schema.json` (now with a description of the arithmetic) and `design_process.md` — are updated together, and `TestNormalizeNativeProcessSettingsDefaultsAndIgnoresBash` asserts the value. **Behaviour change:** a session whose `stdout`/`stderr` budgets add up to more than 8 MiB per stream and that relied on the old default cap now gets the existing config error `tools.native.process stream budgets must not exceed log_cap_bytes`; naming `log_cap_bytes` restores any larger value up to the 1 GiB maximum. A stream that spills past 8 MiB stops writing to its log file earlier than before (it is marked capped, and the produced-bytes count is unchanged); the status budgets the model reads (`stdout`/`stderr`) are unaffected. Nothing prunes faster or slower.
- **An async poll indexes the tail of a transcript, not the whole of it (P6BM-15).** `activityLatest` rebuilt the turn index of every live agent handle from offset 0 on every `async wait`/`list`/`status` — one `json.Unmarshal` per line, up to `tools.native.async.latest_scan_max_bytes` (8 MiB) — and only then walked the news from the cursor's turn. PH1 measured 35.4 ms per 1 MiB handle, 276.6 ms at the cap, 356 ms for ten handles, and the same cost whether or not anything had been appended. The `activityProgress` cursor now also holds the turn its offset falls in (`turnOff`, `turn`), and the index resumes at that turn start (`indexTranscriptTurnsFrom`), numbering the turns exactly as a scan of the whole file would: `session_turn` still wins over the count, which is why a compacted or reset transcript stays right. The whole index still runs on the first poll of a handle, on a cursor that was only seeded (`seedActivity`), when the recorded turn start is no longer a line start, and when the file is shorter than the cursor — a stale cursor therefore cannot parse from the middle of a line. The tail pass already started at that turn, so the block text is byte-identical; a poll with nothing appended reports back the offset its cursor holds instead of falling to 0, which would have shown the same steps twice. Tests: `TestIndexTranscriptTurnsFromResumesAtATurnStart`, `TestResumeTranscriptTurnsFallsBackFromAStaleCursor`, `TestLatestActivityIndexesOnlyTheTailAppendedSinceItsCursor` (it rewrites the head of the transcript in place, same length, so a poll that indexed from 0 would number the step differently).
- **A close signals all its processes at once (P6BM-16).** `ManagedProcessManager.Close` called `Stop` once per running process, and a `Stop` waits `kill_grace_ms` for SIGTERM and again for SIGKILL, so a storage directory at the default `max_running` 8 whose children ignore SIGTERM held the close for about 16 s (PH3: 1.606 s serial against 202.6 ms concurrent, 7.9x, at a 200 ms grace). The stops now run concurrently and the close waits for all of them, so it costs one grace; each `Stop` still owns its entry and its failure is reported in the id order the map walk gave. Test: `TestCloseSignalsEveryRunningProcessAtOnce`.
- **A block the total budget cuts keeps its newest bytes (P6BM-05).** `latestBlocks` cut a block that overran the remaining `latest_total_bytes` from its end, so the second of two handles lost exactly the news it had not shown yet, and it stored the collector's full offsets as the cursor: the bytes were never offered again. It now keeps the tail and marks what it left out with the same "…+N bytes earlier" line `outputLatest` uses, and the marker counts against `latest_total_bytes`, so one response still adds no more than the setting allows (a budget smaller than the marker keeps the newest bytes alone). The cursor still moves, as the function documents, and the full result still comes back from the handle on `wait`. Test: `TestLatestTotalIsCappedAcrossHandles`, whose fixture is now 60 x then 40 Z — all-x text could not tell the two ends apart.

## Phase 6c: sub-agent run fixes (Go only, 2026-11-02)

Audit: `audit_findings/p6c_merged.md` (batch (i): P6CM-04, 07, 02, 08).

- **A restored run's answer is kept whole, whoever reads it first (P6CM-07).** `loadText` read the transcript once but cut the answer to the first caller's limit and set `cut`, and `Detail` passed the async status limit (4096), so one `async status` after a restart left 4 KiB of a 21 KB answer for the life of the process and told every later reader it was cut. A reader that arrived during the first read also saw the flag set and got an empty text. The read is now a `sync.Once` (a second reader waits for the first), stores the whole text and leaves `cut` as the manifest had it; `Detail` already cuts what it returns. No schema or text change. Test: `TestSubagentRestoredAnswerIsNotPinnedToTheFirstReadersLimit`.
